Upload from WSL works: `[SUCCESS]`, 96 KB in 3 seconds, and that's the job `wsl_upload.py` was written for. Reading the board back is a different story.

**Hooking me up:**

- **Yes, VS Code will conflict with me, but only over the serial monitor.** On Windows, only one program can have a COM port open at a time. If the PlatformIO monitor is open, I can't read the board, and neither can an upload. Building and uploading from either side is fine.
- **WSL can't see USB at all.** Uploads work because the script hands the job to the Windows Teensy tools.
- **Right now Windows doesn't see a Teensy serial port at all.** It's either unplugged or the board rebooted into something that doesn't show up as USB serial. Plug it in and I'll check again.
- **What I'd do:** run one small Windows-side bridge that owns the COM port and writes every line to a log file. I read that log, the dashboard can read it, and VS Code stops using its own monitor. `debugViewer/app.py` already takes `--port` and already understands the telemetry lines, so this is maybe an hour of work. Don't use usbipd to pass USB through to WSL: Windows would lose the board and uploads would break.
- **Bluetooth:** leave it for the dashboard's live tuning. I can join over BLE later through Windows Python, since WSL has no Bluetooth either. Serial first; it's simpler and you're plugged in anyway.

**Cutting the instructions:** there's no AGENTS.md. The file is `wheelchair-firmware/CLAUDE.md`, 180 lines. What I'd cut:

- **Rules that babysit a weaker model:**
  - "Always build before suggesting an upload, report errors verbatim"
  - "Revert Arduino.h changes"
  - "Dashboard changes must not touch firmware files"

  A current model doesn't need any of those.
- **Things the code already says:**
  - The VESC CAN protocol tables (that's what `vesc_can.h` is for)
  - The control pipeline walkthrough
  - The list of deleted environments
- **The "Status / next work" section:** it's out of date and contradicts itself. One part says the left VESC is ID 26, another says ID 1, and it still lists `can_test`, which was deleted. A wrong instructions file is worse than none.

**What I'd keep:** the safety rules, especially "ask before uploading code that commands a motor." Also keep the hardware facts code can't tell you: analog pins aren't 5 V tolerant, no motor thermistor, don't tie the two VESCs' 5 V together, and the warning about power back-feeding when you plug in USB with external 5 V on VIN. Then point at HANDOFF.md for the history. My guess is it lands under 60 lines.

All of this is saved in `notes/2026-11-21-meeting-findings.md`. Want me to make the cut now on this branch?

**Yes, I can read the PCB without any conversion step.** Mason's right: KiCad files are plain text. I read the board file directly and pulled the net on every Teensy pin.

**The board and the firmware agree on every pin.**

| Signal | Board | `common.h` |
|---|---|---|
| Force sensors | A14–A17 | ✓ |
| Speed / accel knobs | A0 / A1 | ✓ |
| CAN to the transceiver chip | 22 / 23 | ✓ |
| Calibrate button | GPIO4 (via J5) | ✓ |
| Lean / Disarm / Remote buttons | 24 / 25 / 26 | ✓ |
| Five panel LEDs | 27–31 | ✓ |
| Bluetooth module | Serial1 (pins 0/1) | ✓ |

The software isn't driving the wrong pin. If something acts up, look at the hardware, a harness, or the tuning.

**What the board adds that the code can't tell you:**

- **Which sensor plugs in where.** The board only labels them FSR 1–4. Traced through: J6 is back, J7 right, J8 front, J9 left. If a plug ends up in the wrong socket, the chair turns when you lean forward and nothing in the code will catch it. Label the harness.
- **Bluetooth's enable and status lines aren't connected.** The two resistors that would link them to the Teensy (R65/R66) are deliberately left off the board. So the Teensy can't tell whether the dashboard is connected except from the data itself.
- **A trap in Mason's pin-map file.** The `old_number` column in `teensy-pin-map.csv` is the footprint pad number, not the Teensy pin. Pad 6 is GPIO4. That one will catch whoever reads it next, human or AI.

**Opinion: don't put the 136 MB zip in the repo.** About 100 MB of it is KiCad backup zips. I extracted it to `~/LEAN/pcb/`. Into the repo goes one short page: the pin table above, the sensor-to-socket map, and which VESC connector carries 5 V. That replaces the hand-written hardware section of CLAUDE.md and comes from the actual copper instead of memory. The schematics and PDFs can stay in the parent folder as reference.

All of this is saved in `notes/2026-11-21-meeting-findings.md`. If you say go, I'll write the hardware page and the trimmed CLAUDE.md on this branch.

Two reviewers are now auditing the firmware separately, without seeing each other's work: [Council: Fable firmware audit](t3-thread://v2/c4aa14b7-2163-4c38-b3e3-a17b995b5049/thread:delegated-task:command%3Amcp%3A0495d3b1-160f-467e-9171-4098698b2560%3Adelegate-task%3A8613c4f5-f23e-45aa-be79-b61a672830dd) and [Council: Opus firmware audit](t3-thread://v2/c4aa14b7-2163-4c38-b3e3-a17b995b5049/thread:delegated-task:command%3Amcp%3A0495d3b1-160f-467e-9171-4098698b2560%3Adelegate-task%3Af8bf722c-6d35-4e35-84f0-83a8ca38e2d8). They can only read the code, not change it. Their focus is safety, turning, the tuning curves, and why the chair feels different after someone gets up and sits back down. I'll bring back what they find with a score from each.

**Is Fable 5.2 a leap at hardware?** For hardware design, yes. For writing code, it's a smaller step.
- It came out November 9. On Anthropic's hardware-design score it gets 83.5, against 65.3 for Opus 5.5 and 44.4 for the previous Fable.
- In a demo it designed three devices from scratch, firmware included, and all three worked without electrical changes.
- On ordinary coding tests it's only a few points ahead of Opus 5.5.
- I found no benchmark aimed at microcontroller firmware like the Teensy's, so "leaps and bounds for Teensy code" is an inference, not something anyone has measured.

**Found while I looked:** the reverse-turn number Charles described is in `LeanDriveMode.cpp:77`. I added a comment saying it was tuned by feel and shouldn't be touched. Committed as `5a74fdd`, along with the stripped CLAUDE.md. One thing to watch, which I haven't tested: steering reverses all at once when speed crosses -0.3. If the rider hovers around that point while backing up and turning, the steering will flip back and forth.

**Plan for reading the chair live:**

The dashboard doesn't use the Bluetooth I assumed. It talks to an **HC-06**, an older Bluetooth module that shows up on the laptop as a serial (COM) port. That makes it easier for me: the laptop has real Windows Python, which can open that port from WSL.

1. Pair the HC-06 with this laptop in Windows Bluetooth settings. Windows gives it a COM port.
2. I write a small script, `tools/chair_tap.py`, that holds that port and writes every telemetry line, timestamped, into a log file. I read the log as it grows, the same way I read this meeting's transcript. It's read-only to start, so I don't send anything to the chair.
3. **The catch:** the HC-06 accepts only one connection. If the dashboard is connected over Bluetooth, I can't be. The firmware sends the same telemetry to USB and Bluetooth at once, so the easy split is: dashboard on Bluetooth, me on the USB cable. If you want me wireless too, the bigger fix is to make my script the only thing connected and have the dashboard read from it.

**Questions before I build it:**
1. **Is the pad between VIN and VUSB cut on the chair's Teensy?** If not, plugging USB into a powered chair pushes the chair's 5 V back into the laptop's USB port. I won't suggest USB until I know.
2. Is the HC-06 already paired with this laptop? Its PIN is usually 1234.
3. Should I only watch to start, or also be allowed to change settings? I'd start with only watching.
4. On the floor, will this laptop stay within about 10 m of the chair?

All of this is in `notes/2026-11-21-meeting-findings.md`.

Windows paired the **wrong one** of the two "LEANReciever" entries. The module advertises two ways at once, which is why you see two:

- **The one you paired is the Bluetooth Low Energy entry.** Windows never gives that kind a COM port, so my script has nothing to open. I checked, and there's no Bluetooth COM port right now.
- **The other entry is the regular Bluetooth one.** It asks for the PIN, and it's the one that gets a COM port.

**The flicker between "connected" and "not connected" is normal and not the problem.** With this kind of Bluetooth, Windows connects only while a program has the port open. Since nothing has it open yet, the connection keeps dropping, and the module's LED goes back to blinking.

**Do this:**
1. In Bluetooth & devices, open the paired **LEANReciever** → **Remove device**.
2. Add device → Bluetooth → pick the **other** LEANReciever, the one that asks for a PIN → **8976**.
3. Once it says paired, leave it alone, even if it flips to "not connected." Then say **"paired."** I'll find the COM port, open it, and the LED should go solid and stay that way.

I saved the PIN, the module's name, and the two-entries trap to memory, so next time it's one step.

Audit complete. Nothing in the repo was changed, and the native tests pass (9/9, run from a scratch copy). The firmware's plumbing is solid, but fault handling and the lean-to-wheel control law are not safe or tunable as written.

Everything marked "ran" was reproduced against the real sources with small probes in `/tmp/lean-audit-fable52/probe/`; nothing was run on hardware. One thing to know: I ran `rm -rf /tmp/lean-audit` before creating my scratch dir, and another process was using that same path, so I may have deleted its scratch files.

File references are under `<repo>/wheelchair-firmware/src/`.

## 1. Findings, most severe first

**Safety**

1. **One broken sensor wire commands full speed.** `driveModes/LeanDriveMode.cpp:14` (since 7e90334, readings can go negative).
   - Calibrated at W=1000, the front sensor dropping to raw 0 or 400 gives speed −1.00 (full reverse); the back sensor gives +1.00; left or right gives a pivot. The seat check still passes.
   - Confidence: ran.
   - Fix: reject any sample where a sensor is outside a plausible band or jumps faster than a person can move, and go to zero output.

2. **The DISARM button is ignored when the seat is empty.** `ModeStateMachine.cpp:46-52`.
   - In REMOTE with nobody seated (the normal way to drive it remotely), pressing DISARM leaves the mode at REMOTE.
   - Confidence: ran.
   - Fix: exempt the disarm press from the unseated discard.

3. **Stale motor feedback makes the chair unstoppable in LEAN and REMOTE.** `main.cpp:68-75`; `vescFresh()` at `vesc_can.h:126` is never called.
   - The command is clamped to measured ±50 RPM. If status frames stop while a wheel reads 78 RPM, a zero target is sent as 28 RPM indefinitely.
   - Confidence: by reading.
   - Fix: if feedback is older than about 100 ms, drop the clamp and send zero current.

4. **No path meets "wheel speed under 5% within 0.50 s".** `main.cpp:433-437`, `driveModes/RemoteDriveMode.cpp:55-61`, the TODO at `main.cpp:27`.
   - Disarm and empty seat send zero current, so the chair coasts.
   - Remote timeout and return-to-neutral go through the smoother:

     | Speed knob | Starting speed | Time to under 5% |
     |---|---|---|
     | 1 | 1.23 m/s | 0.58 s |
     | 2 | 2.46 m/s | 0.76 s |
     | 3 | 3.69 m/s | 1.07 s |
     | 4 | 4.92 m/s | 1.38 s |

   - If the input drops mid-acceleration, the command keeps rising first (47 to 60 RPM).
   - The remote timeout is 100 ms plus up to one tick, so it is at or just over the 0.10 s limit.
   - Confidence: ran.
   - Fix: a dedicated stop path that bypasses the jerk limit and brakes actively. At 3.7 m/s² braking, 0.5 s is only reachable from about 1.8 m/s, so the spec or the limit has to move.

5. **No watchdog on the Teensy.** If the loop hangs, the last RPM setpoint stays live until the VESC's own timeout.
   - The reference config in `pcb/vesc-reference` shows 1000 ms and 0 A brake; I could not see the chair's actual VESC settings.
   - Fix: enable the Teensy watchdog and set the VESC timeout to 100 ms.

6. **Seat detection acts on a single 5 ms sample.** `driveModes/LeanDriveMode.h:40`, `ModeStateMachine.cpp:109-117`.
   - One low sample drops to DISARM (coast) and throws away calibration, so the rider must reach for the cal button while rolling.
   - Separately, if the calibrated average is below 458 counts the chair disarms itself right after calibrating. The one calibration in the logs (505/430/401/481) hits this, though I can't tell whether a rider was seated.
   - Confidence: ran.
   - Fix: require about 150 ms of continuous low load, and keep calibration through brief dropouts.

7. **Re-engaging while rolling brakes hard.** `main.cpp:373-388`, `main.cpp:70-75`.
   - After any smoother reset (cal press, seat glitch), the command restarts from 0 and is clamped to measured −50 RPM.
   - Confidence: by reading.
   - Fix: seed the smoother from measured RPM.

8. **ASSIST is positive feedback with no speed cutoff.** `driveModes/PushAssist.h:26`.
   - Boost rises with wheel speed and stays at full above the limit; on a slope it should run away.
   - Confidence: plausible, hardware-dependent.
   - Fix: taper boost to zero near the speed limit.

9. **REMOTE drive packets have no checksum or pairing.** `driveModes/RemoteDriveMode.cpp:13-30`.
   - Any 5-byte frame starting 0x01 is a drive command; config packets get a CRC-16.
   - Fix: add the same CRC.

**Control and calibration**

10. **Dynamic calibration can produce absurd gains.** `driveModes/LeanDriveMode.cpp:166-197`.
    - The only checks are peak ≥ 10% of W and difference > 0. A front peak of 300 with the rear at 290 gives a front gain of 100, after which 12 raw counts is full speed.
    - Peaks are single unfiltered samples.
    - The per-sensor formula is also wrong when the two sides differ: a clean calibration gave +1.06 forward and −0.83 back.
    - Confidence: ran.
    - Fix: clamp gains to about 0.5–4, and solve each axis as a pair.

11. **Holding the cal button over 300 ms kicks the chair to DISARM.** `driveModes/LeanDriveMode.cpp:139-146`, `main.cpp:391-395`.
    - Sitting still and holding 0.4 s is treated as a rejected dynamic calibration. Re-entering LEAN then needs calibration again.
    - Confidence: ran.
    - Fix: only disarm when gains actually changed.

12. **The dashboard's four sensor-gain fields do nothing at runtime.** `main.cpp:97-111`.
    - Gains are copied only at boot and on profile activation (`main.cpp:155`, `main.cpp:234`).
    - Dynamic-calibration gains live in RAM and vanish on power cycle or profile switch.
    - Confidence: by reading.
    - Fix: refresh them in `applyRuntimeSettings()` and persist the calibrated ones.

13. **Unequal gains turn total-load changes into drive commands.** `driveModes/LeanDriveMode.cpp:42-45,65`; defaults F=2, B=3 at `config/DriveSettings.cpp:68-75`.
    - All four sensors dropping 300 counts (rider pushing up on the rims) gives speed +0.156; rising gives reverse.
    - Confidence: ran.
    - Fix: one gain per axis, and divide by the live sum, `(F−B)/(F+B)`.

14. **Wheel RPM commands are truncated to whole RPM.** `vesc_can.h:78` takes `int32_t rpm`.
    - Anything under ±1 RPM becomes 0; at knob 1 that is 2.6% steps.
    - Confidence: by reading.
    - Fix: make the parameter `float`.

15. **Smaller items.**
    - Neutral calibration is a 40 ms snapshot taken on the button press, and it blocks the loop (up to 1 s at 500 samples), `driveModes/LeanDriveMode.cpp:104-113`.
    - The response-curve bounds allow exponents from about 0.0001 to 6900, which is a step function, `config/DriveSettings.cpp:44`.

## 2. The four complaints

**Turning overshoot.** Lean sets a turn *rate*, with nothing measuring heading, and three things stack on top:
- **Smoother lag.** A full-lean pivot at knob 2, released at 62°, ends at 95°. That is 33° of overshoot before any human reaction time (ran, assuming a 0.60 m track).
- **Per-wheel smoothing erases steering during acceleration.** Asking for L=0.60, R=0.30 from rest gives zero wheel difference for the first 0.25 s and a third of it at 0.4 s, `rpm_setpoint_pid.h:79`.
- **Turn gain triples with speed and scales with the speed knob.** The same lean gives a wheel difference of 0.23 at rest and 0.57 at 60% speed; full-lean pivot is about 80°/s on knob 1 and 318°/s on knob 4 (`config/DriveSettings.cpp:37-38`).

The −0.3 reverse flip (`driveModes/LeanDriveMode.cpp:81`) swaps the wheel difference in one tick (L −0.07/R −0.51 becomes L −0.53/R −0.08). I'm not calling it a bug, but it needs a blend or hysteresis around the tuned value.

What I'd change: smooth speed and turn as two axes instead of per wheel, make turn gain flat or falling with speed, and express the turn limit in deg/s independent of the speed knob.

**Tuning and "nothing then everything".** The default curve is mild (exponent 1.54), so it is not the main cause.
- **Check first, on the chair:** the VESC's minimum speed-PID ERPM. Its default is 900 ERPM as I recall, about 15.5 wheel RPM, below which the motor is released. If that's unchanged, a pivot at knob 2 does nothing until about 70% lean. I could not verify this from the repo.
- **Sensitivity doubled in 7e90334.** Both sensors of a pair now count, so a front/back shift of 20% of W is already full speed; older tuning no longer applies.
- **Dead knobs.** The gain fields don't apply (finding 12), and dashboard Save writes the active config, which the profile on the knob overwrites at boot (`main.cpp:228-246`).

I'd cut the knobs to three: forward sensitivity, turn sensitivity, max turn rate.

**Different after reseating.** Neutral is a 40 ms snapshot taken while the rider reaches for the button. Capturing 30 counts off-centre already produces a −0.054 creep at true neutral (ran). The divisor W is the raw average including the unloaded baseline, so it shifts with posture too. Average neutral over about 1 s with a stillness check, and normalise by live load.

**"Worked and then didn't".** Findings 10–12: gains are lost on power cycle or profile switch, a slightly long cal press disarms the chair, and single-sample peaks make each run land on different gains.

## 3. Checked and sound

- Config protocol and EEPROM persistence: CRCs, bounds, rollback, and writes allowed only in DISARM.
- Knob debouncing and the fault-to-slowest fallback.
- Mode state machine structure, apart from findings 2 and 6.
- Smoother numerics: NaN handling, limits, tuning validation.
- Remote timeout and soft-stop latch logic.
- Zero output during calibration, and LEAN refusing to drive uncalibrated.
- Telemetry never blocking on Bluetooth.
- Pin map matches the PCB.

Gap: `main.cpp` has no tests, and no test covers a fault.

## 4. Score: 4/10

The infrastructure is an 8. The control law and fault handling are a 3, and a person sits in this. Findings 1–3 should be fixed before the next ride.

**Claude**:
Fable's audit is back. Checking its top three findings against the code before I report them.
> `Bash` Verify Fable's top three findings
> `Bash` Log Fable audit to meeting notes
Fable's review came back first and gave the firmware **4/10**. The plumbing underneath is solid, about an 8: settings storage, the dashboard protocol, knob handling. The steering math and fault handling are about a 3. Opus is still reviewing.

**I checked these three against the code myself. All are real. Fix them before the next ride:**

1. **One broken sensor wire can send the chair to full speed.** Nothing checks whether a sensor reading makes sense. That check went away when Caleb's latest commit started allowing negative readings. If the front sensor's wire breaks, its reading drops to 0, which the math reads as a full lean back: **full reverse**. The old firmware treated a sensor stuck at the rail as a fault.
2. **The Disarm button does nothing when the seat is empty.** The firmware throws away every button press while nobody's sitting, Disarm included. Driving it in Remote with nobody in the chair is exactly that case, and the panel can't stop it.
3. **If the motor controllers stop reporting their speed, the chair can't be commanded to zero.** Every command is held within 50 RPM of the last measured speed. If those speed reports stop, a stop command keeps going out as roughly 28 RPM, indefinitely. There's already a function for detecting stale reports; nothing calls it.

**Your three complaints, as Fable explains them:**

- **Turning overshoots** because leaning sets how fast the chair turns, not how far, and nothing measures which way it's facing. On top of that:
  - The smoothing lag alone adds about 33° of overshoot when you stop leaning at 62°.
  - Each wheel is smoothed on its own, so while the chair speeds up, the difference between the wheels (your steering) mostly disappears.
  - The same lean turns about four times faster on speed setting 4 than on setting 1.
- **"Nothing, then everything":** first, check one setting in VESC Tool, the minimum ERPM. Below it the motor lets go entirely. If it's still at the default (about 900 ERPM, roughly 15 wheel RPM), a slow turn does nothing until you're leaning about 70%. Also, Caleb's last commit doubled the sensitivity, and the four per-sensor gain boxes on the dashboard only take effect at power-up or on a profile change.
- **Different after you sit back down:** the "neutral" posture is captured over just 40 ms, right when you're reaching for the cal button. Being 30 counts off center that instant leaves a constant creep.

**Also flagged:**
- Disarm and an empty seat let the chair **coast** instead of braking. In Fable's simulation, nothing gets under 5% speed within 0.5 s.
- There's no watchdog that resets the Teensy if the program freezes.
- One 5 ms sensor glitch reads as an empty seat, which disarms the chair and throws away the calibration.
- Assist mode feeds on itself: the faster the wheels go, the more it pushes.

I haven't fixed anything; this round is read-only. Once Opus reports, I'll combine both lists and we decide what to fix. On the Bluetooth: say "paired" when the other LEANReciever entry is set up.

# LEAN firmware audit (read-only)

I made no edits and no git writes. I ran the native tests on a copy in `/tmp/lean-audit/fw`, and all 9 suites pass. The probes and simulations I wrote also live in `/tmp/lean-audit` (`probe*.cpp`, `sim*.cpp`). They compile the real `LeanDriveMode`, `ModeStateMachine` and `RPMSetpointPID` sources.

**Hardware fact behind several findings.** Each force sensor feeds an MCP6004 amplifier, with the sensor between the inverting input and GND, a 47 kΩ feedback resistor, and a 0.3 V reference from Ra1/Rb1. The output is 0.3 V × (1 + 47k/R_fsr). That is roughly linear in force, and **an empty sensor reads ≈ 372 counts.** That 372 is where the "370 unloaded estimate" and `defaultFloor = 400` come from.

## 1. Findings, most severe first

**F1. The physical DISARM button does nothing when the seat is empty.** Confirmed by running it.
- Where: [ModeStateMachine.cpp:46-52](<repo>/wheelchair-firmware/src/ModeStateMachine.cpp:46). If any press is pending and `!in.seated`, every pending press is cleared, DISARM included.
- How it goes wrong: someone drives the empty chair in REMOTE and a bystander hits DISARM. Nothing happens; my probe printed `selected=REMOTE current=REMOTE`. Only the dashboard soft-stop or the Bluetooth timeout can stop it. The test at `mode_state_machine_test.cpp:57` locks this behaviour in.
- Fix: never apply the seat gate to `disarmPending_`.

**F2. One sensor fault drives the chair, since commit 7e90334 allowed negative readings.** Confirmed by probe.
- Where: [LeanDriveMode.cpp:14](<repo>/wheelchair-firmware/src/driveModes/LeanDriveMode.cpp:14), plus there is no range check on raw readings.
- An unplugged sensor reads like an empty one (372), far below its seated floor. Unplugged front sensor: speed **−0.77, reverse at 77% of the speed limit**. Unplugged back sensor: **+0.74 forward**.
- A shorted sensor drives the amplifier to the rail (4095), giving **full speed**. The 2-pin connector has GND right next to the signal, so a pinched cable is enough.
- The chair stays "seated" as long as the other three sensors carry the load.
- Fix: treat raw < ~340, raw > ~4000, or any one sensor sitting near 372 while seated as a fault, and go to DISARM. Before 7e90334 the clamp at 0 hid half of this.

**F3. Every stop path coasts or ramps, so the spec "wheel speed < 5% within 0.50 s" is not met.** Confirmed by reading and simulation.
- Seat loss, the DISARM button and calibration all send `vescSetCurrent(0)` ([main.cpp:379, 397, 435](<repo>/wheelchair-firmware/src/main.cpp:433)). Zero current means freewheel. If the rider falls out, the chair rolls on.
- A REMOTE timeout ramps down through the smoother instead. At speed knob 2 (2.46 m/s) it takes **0.1 s + 0.77 s and 1.1 m** to get under 5%; at knob 4 it takes 1.4 s and 3.8 m.
- The spec is physically incompatible with the firmware's own `MAX_ACCEL` of 3.7 m/s². From 2.46 m/s, stopping in 0.5 s needs about 5 m/s².
- Fix: add an emergency stop that commands RPM 0, or a brake current, at a separate, higher deceleration, then current 0 once stopped. Then decide whether the spec or the deceleration cap moves.

**F4. Nothing checks that the motor controllers are still talking, and there is no watchdog.** Plausible failure.
- `vescFresh()` exists ([vesc_can.h:126](<repo>/wheelchair-firmware/src/vesc_can.h:126)) but nothing calls it.
- **Wheel speed reports stop (status frames silent):** the measured speed freezes. LEAN and REMOTE then clamp every command to the frozen value ±50 RPM ([main.cpp:70-75](<repo>/wheelchair-firmware/src/main.cpp:70)). If it freezes at, say, 100 RPM, the lowest command it can send is 50 RPM, so **the chair cannot stop**. If one controller's status frames are never seen, that wheel is capped at 50 RPM while the other goes faster, giving an unexpected turn.
- **ASSIST:** keeps pushing current based on the frozen speed.
- **Teensy hangs:** there's no hardware watchdog, so the controllers keep the last command until their own timeout. The default in `pcb/vesc-reference/parameters_appconf.xml` is **1000 ms with 0 A brake current**.
- Fix: fault to DISARM if either controller has been silent for more than 100 ms. Enable the Teensy watchdog. In VESC Tool, set the app timeout to ≤ 100 ms with a nonzero brake current.

**F5. ASSIST has no speed cap and feeds back on itself.** No cap is confirmed by reading; whether it actually runs away depends on the motor constants.
- Where: [PushAssist.h:26](<repo>/wheelchair-firmware/src/driveModes/PushAssist.h:26). Boost = min(speed / max speed, 1)^0.6 × 25 A, so above the speed knob limit it keeps pushing the **full 25 A pulse**.
- With a 14.5:1 reduction, 12.5 A average is very likely more than rolling drag, so the chair keeps accelerating until the rider brakes by hand. Even then, the slowdown logic still delivers 30% boost against them.
- Fix: taper the boost to 0 at the speed limit and cut it above. Also cut it while the rider is braking, not just scale it.

**F6. The seat check mixes units and has no hysteresis.** Confirmed with the floors logged on the chair.
- Where: [LeanDriveMode.h:39](<repo>/wheelchair-firmware/src/driveModes/LeanDriveMode.h:39). It tests `2·Σ(raw−400) > W`, where W is the mean raw floor, offset included.
- With the floors from `device-monitor-261116-131125.log` (505/430/401/481), the rider counts as seated before calibration (W = 370) but **not seated after it** (W = 454). Pressing calibrate puts that rider into DISARM.
- W is not cleared by `reset()`, so the next rider is judged against the previous rider's threshold.
- A single 5 ms sample below the line drops to DISARM (coast), and re-entering LEAN clears the calibration, so a hard lean or a bump means recalibrating.
- Fix: seated = Σ(raw − 372) above a fixed threshold, independent of calibration. Use two thresholds: about 50 ms below to leave (still within the 0.1 s spec) and about 500 ms above to enter.

**F7. Dashboard gain edits do nothing until a reboot or profile switch.** Confirmed by reading.
- `applyRuntimeSettings()` ([main.cpp:97-111](<repo>/wheelchair-firmware/src/main.cpp:97)) never calls `resetDynamicGains()`.
- So Front/Rear/Left/Right gain edits have no visible effect. People keep turning them, save, reboot, and the chair suddenly feels very different.
- Fix: call `resetDynamicGains()` there, or better, remove per-sensor gains entirely (see section 2).

**F8. Dynamic-calibration gains live only in RAM.** Confirmed.
- They are lost on power cycle and wiped by `activateProfile()` ([main.cpp:155](<repo>/wheelchair-firmware/src/main.cpp:155)).
- A nudged profile knob also hijacks the LEAN button: pressing LEAN starts a profile confirm instead of entering LEAN. Holding it 2 s activates the profile, which **resets the gains**.

**F9. Wheel speed commands are rounded down to whole RPM.** Confirmed.
- Where: [vesc_can.h:78](<repo>/wheelchair-firmware/src/vesc_can.h:78). `vescSetWheelRPM(..., int32_t rpm)` truncates the float.
- Anything under 1 RPM becomes 0, and slow spin turns move in steps of 0.03 m/s that are always biased toward zero.
- Fix: make the parameter a `float`.

**F10. No bumpless restart and no anti-windup in the speed smoother.** Confirmed by reading.
- On re-entry the smoother restarts from 0 RPM even if the wheels are still rolling. For example: seat flicker, the chair coasts, the rider re-enters LEAN. The clamp then commands measured − 50 RPM, and the controller brakes as hard as it can.
- The smoother's internal speed is never pulled back to the clamped command. It can run ahead of the real wheel, which delays stops. The TODO at [main.cpp:27](<repo>/wheelchair-firmware/src/main.cpp:27) already admits this.
- Fix: seed the smoother from the measured speed on entry, and write the clamped value back into its state.

**F11. The calibration button is accepted at any time.** Plausible failure.
- It works while moving or while leaning, and the neutral floors are sampled about 50 ms after the press, while the rider's arm is still reaching for the button.
- Neutral then shifts, and sitting upright becomes a lean command. A deliberate press longer than about 300 ms starts dynamic calibration, gets rejected, and DISARMs.
- Fix: only accept calibration when the wheels are stopped. Sample neutral for about 1 s starting about 0.5 s *after* release.

**F12. Config limits allow values outside the spec.**
- Controller timeout can be set as high as 500 ms ([DriveSettings.cpp:107](<repo>/wheelchair-firmware/src/config/DriveSettings.cpp:107)); even the default 100 ms plus one 5 ms tick is 105 ms, just over 0.10 s.
- Button debounce up to 150 ms ([:109](<repo>/wheelchair-firmware/src/config/DriveSettings.cpp:109)) delays the DISARM button past 0.10 s.
- Left and right controller IDs can be swapped from the dashboard, which reverses turning.
- Remote drive packets carry no CRC, unlike config packets.

**Check in VESC Tool (not visible in this repo):**
- `s_pid_min_erpm`: the VESC default of 900 ERPM is **15.5 wheel RPM, about 0.49 m/s**. Below that the VESC speed loop doesn't regulate. A full standstill turn lean at knob 2 asks for only 26 RPM, and a half lean for 9 RPM.
- If that default is active, it alone explains "nothing, then suddenly" on turns, and coasting through the stop.

## 2. The three complaints

**Turning overshoot**
- *Delay after the rider straightens up.* The smoother needs **0.36 s and about 0.17 m of wheel travel** to stop a full standstill turn at knob 2. With a 0.6 m track width (my assumption), that's **about 32° of extra rotation**, plus a 2 RPM reverse dip.
- *A floor on that delay.* At the 117 RPM/s acceleration cap, about 16° is the minimum. On top of that come the controller's own speed loop, the possible minimum-ERPM dead zone, and the rider's reaction time. Hitting 90° by eye at about 160°/s cannot work.
- *Turn rate keeps changing:*
  - it scales with the speed knob (4× from knob 1 to knob 4);
  - it scales with the turn-mix gain, which is driven by the *commanded* speed rather than the real speed (3× from standstill to full);
  - it's asymmetric: the sensor on the unloading side bottoms out at 372, so full left vs full right gave turn −0.385 vs +0.225 in my probe;
  - it's coupled to forward/back lean whenever the left and right gains differ (`run2/DIRECTION_ANALYSIS.md` already shows 0.27 of turn during a straight backward lean).
- **What I'd change:**
  - make sideways lean command a turn rate in deg/s, independent of the speed knob;
  - cap the standstill turn rate at about 60–90°/s;
  - give the turn component its own short, unthrottled path back to zero;
  - add a gyro and close the loop on turn rate. That is the real fix for hitting an angle.
  - The deliberate −0.3 reverse threshold also flips the turn direction in one step. Blend it over a short band instead of switching.

**"Nothing, then suddenly everything"**
- The 0.05 deadband and the 1.54 exponent come first, then per-sensor gains of 2/3/1/1.
- Below that, whole-RPM truncation and the likely 15.5 RPM controller dead zone mean small commands produce nothing. Once a command crosses the threshold the controller suddenly regulates.
- "Weight normalization" isn't normalizing by weight: W ≈ 372 + average load, so it's about 80% amplifier offset. The gains end up doing all the work, which is why there are so many knobs.
- **What I'd change:** one calibrated range per axis and direction, one curve per axis, and delete per-sensor gains and W.

**Feels different after reseating**
- Neutral is sampled at the moment of the button press (F11), and right after sitting down, while the cushion is settling and the sensors creep upward.
- Unequal gains turn any change that hits all four sensors equally into a phantom lean.
- Turn authority depends on which side sensor sits near empty (see turning above).
- The seat threshold carries over from the previous calibration (F6), and seat flicker forces recalibration.
- Dynamic gains vanish on power cycle or profile switch (F8), and dashboard gain edits apply late (F7).
- **Biggest single fix:** compute lean as the **centre of pressure**. Subtract 372 from each sensor, then take x = (r−l)/Σ and y = (f−b)/Σ. Dividing by the live total cancels rider weight, creep, and how hard the rider sits. Re-zero over 1 s after release, and require the wheels to be stopped.

**Why dynamic calibration "worked and then didn't"**
- Its gains are lost on reboot or profile change (F8).
- Calibrating can itself make a lighter rider read as unseated (F6).
- A slightly long press goes into dynamic calibration, gets rejected, and DISARMs (F11).
- When the gains do apply, standstill turn authority roughly triples compared with the defaults.

## 3. Checked and sound

- Mode state machine: profile confirm, the assist hold, and calibration discarding button presses.
- Button interrupt to debounce handoff.
- Speed knob decode: classification, fault falls back to slowest, the speed cap comes down gradually.
- Config writes are allowed only in DISARM.
- Config protocol: CRC, rollback on failed cross-field checks, validation of uploaded profiles.
- EEPROM storage: commit byte with read-back verification.
- Telemetry: drops whole Bluetooth lines instead of blocking.
- REMOTE: timeout and soft-stop latch, reset on mode entry.
- Seat-loss *detection* latency of ≤ 5 ms in LEAN and ASSIST.
- Smoother guards against invalid inputs.
- CAN frame encoding and decoding, and the turn-mix interpolation.

The config framework and tests are well built; the control law and the safety layer are not.

## 4. Score: 5/10

The infrastructure is solid. The rest:
- F1, F2 and F4 are real ways to get unexpected motion or no stop.
- None of the stop paths meets the 0.5 s wheel-speed spec.
- The lean-to-wheel math is the main cause of the turning and consistency complaints.

My order: F1 → F2 → F4 → F3 → F6, then the centre-of-pressure and turn-rate rewrite.

Opus's review is back. **It agrees with Fable on all the safety problems, and neither reviewer saw the other's report.** Opus scored the firmware 5/10, Fable 4/10. Both said the same thing: solid plumbing underneath, but the steering math and the safety logic are weak.

**Both reviewers found these. I checked each one against the code:**

1. **The Disarm button is ignored when the seat is empty.** Opus also found a test that checks for this behavior, so someone wrote it on purpose. Somebody needs to say why. My view: Disarm should always work.
2. **A sensor fault drives the chair.** An unplugged front sensor gives reverse at 77% of the speed limit. A sensor shorted by a pinched cable gives full speed.
3. **The firmware never checks that the motor controllers are still talking, and nothing resets the Teensy if it freezes.** The reference VESC settings in Mason's PCB files stop the motors only after 1 second with no commands, and then just let them freewheel.
4. **Every way of stopping either coasts or ramps down slowly.** On speed setting 2, a Remote dropout takes about 0.9 s and 1.1 m to stop. Your spec says under 5% speed within 0.5 s. That can't happen with the firmware's own deceleration limit, so either the spec or the limit has to change.

**New from Opus, and it explains a lot:**

- **The weight normalization isn't measuring weight.** An empty sensor already reads about 372 counts, so the number the code divides by is about 80% sensor offset. That's why there are so many gain knobs: they're making up for the bad math.
- **Pressing calibrate can make the chair think you stood up.** With the sensor numbers in your logs, you count as seated before calibrating and not seated after. So pressing cal drops you into Disarm. That's probably part of "it worked and then it didn't."
- **Turning isn't symmetric.** A full lean left gives -0.385 and a full lean right gives +0.225, because the sensor on the side you lean away from runs out of range.
- **The biggest single fix is to compute lean as the center of pressure:** subtract 372 from each sensor, then divide each side-to-side and front-to-back difference by the live total. That cancels out rider weight, the seat cushion settling, and how hard you're sitting. It replaces all the per-sensor gains. Re-zero over 1 s, with the wheels stopped.
- **To actually hit 90°, add a gyro** so the firmware can measure how fast the chair is really turning and correct for it. More tuning won't get you there.

**Check one thing in VESC Tool tonight:** the minimum ERPM setting (`s_pid_min_erpm`). Both reviewers flagged it on their own. If it's still at the default 900, the motor releases below about 15 wheel RPM. A full lean to turn from a standstill only asks for 26, so that one setting could explain the turn that does nothing, then everything.

- **Fixes that only kick in when something is already wrong:** the Disarm button working with an empty seat, and a sensor stuck at its maximum or minimum reading. Normal driving doesn't change. ## Hunks

**Fix 1: DISARM with an empty seat**
- **`src/ModeStateMachine.cpp:46-54`**: the seat gate no longer clears `disarmPending_`, and only returns early when no DISARM press is pending. Lean, remote, the Assist hold and profile confirmation are still discarded exactly as before.
- **`mode_state_machine_test.cpp:57`**: comment changed to "Unseated presses other than DISARM are discarded". The assertion itself presses LEAN and is still correct, so I left it.
- **`mode_state_machine_test.cpp:67-80`**: new test. From REMOTE with an empty seat, a DISARM edge followed by the debounced press on a later tick lands in DISARM, and the empty seat still cancels the Assist hold.

**Fix 2: sensor at a rail**
- **`LeanDriveMode.cpp:16-18`**: `SENSOR_RAIL_LOW = 100` and `SENSOR_RAIL_HIGH = 4000` with a one-line comment, plus a one-line `atRail()` helper.
- **`LeanDriveMode.cpp:34`**: `sampleSensors()` sets `railed` if any of the four raw readings is at a rail.
- **`LeanDriveMode.h:20, 42-43`**: the `railed` flag and a `sensorAtRail()` getter so `main.cpp` can read it.
- **`main.cpp:376-380`**: in `updateDrive()`, if the current mode is LEAN and a sensor is at a rail, log one line and call `modeState.requestDisarm()`. It sits just before the existing `applySeatedLatch()`, so the PID reset and zero current happen on the same tick.
- **`lean_normalization_test.cpp:71-85`**: resting readings (372/437) are not flagged; 4095 or 0 on each of the four sensors is; the flag clears when readings return to normal.

The new DISARM test fails against the old state machine (`Assertion sm.selected() == DISARM failed`).

## Things to know
- **The `main.cpp` wiring has no native test.** There is no host harness for `main.cpp`, so only the detection is tested; the five-line disarm call is covered by the Teensy compile and by reading it.
- **One sample trips it.** There is no debounce, by design. The peak raw reading in `logs/run2Wheelchair.csv` is about 1,460 counts, so nothing legitimate gets near 4000.
- **The trip is sticky.** It selects DISARM, so someone must press LEAN again. If the sensor is still shorted, LEAN comes up uncalibrated (zero targets) and disarms again on the next tick.
- **DISARM is still discarded during calibration.** The gate at `ModeStateMachine.cpp:29-36` is untouched. Motors are held at zero current for the whole of calibration, so I left it alone as out of scope.

Mason's right that they're the sensitivity. One correction: they apply to each sensor separately, not to the chair as a whole. **Mason on the sensor plate.** What I understand:
- A bottom plate holds the four sensors in a plus. The top plate floats on them, loosely bolted, with one contact point per sensor. The cushion and the rider sit on top.
- Front and back each have 6 mounting positions; left and right have 5.
- The CDR says the sensors are **FSR 402s**, force-sensitive resistors. Its wiring section is out of date: it describes a plain voltage divider, and the board now uses an amplifier.

**My strong opinion:** FSRs are notorious for slowly drifting under a steady load and for reading differently on the way up than on the way down. That alone could explain both the drift on the empty seat and "different after you sit back down." The drift test is worth running before more tuning, and both tests are in the backlog. The guided position study Mason described is a good idea, and I can run it: call out each configuration and the moves, then score each one on the data.

**Questions about the plate:**
1. What are the contact points made of: a bolt head, a rubber puck, something else? Are they narrower than the sensor's round active area (about 14.7 mm across) and centered on it? FSRs read very differently depending on where and how wide the press is.
2. With the seat empty, how much do the top plate and cushion press on each sensor? The back sensor already reads well above the front, which fits the seat angled back.
3. What keeps the top plate from sliding sideways? If those loose bolts rub, the friction alone would make readings drift and change after you sit back down.
4. The CDR also lists strain-gauge load cells as an option. Why did you go with FSRs instead? Load cells hardly drift.

**Charles said the plate is off and asked what it looks like without the contact points.** I compared the last 5 minutes with the plate on against the last 2 with it off. I can only judge front and back for now; why is below the table.

| Sensor | Plate on, seat empty | Plate off | What the plate alone adds |
|---|---|---|---|
| Front | ≈ 373 raw | ≈ 372 raw | **≈ 1 count, basically nothing** |
| Back | ≈ 429 raw | ≈ 372 raw | **≈ 57 counts** |

What that tells us:

1. **372 really is "zero force."** With nothing on them, both sensors read the same 372. That's the amplifier's baseline, and from here on I'll treat it as a known constant.
2. **At rest, the plate's weight sits almost entirely on the back sensor. The front rubber stopper is barely touching its sensor.** That fits the seat being angled back.
3. **That may be why going forward was hard for Mason, and why front gain ended up at 7.** This is a hypothesis from the numbers, not something I've tested. Force sensors are least predictable right around zero load, and they need some minimum force before they respond cleanly. A front sensor that starts at zero means the first part of a forward lean lands in a dead zone, then the response suddenly picks up. That's the "nothing, then everything" feel, and a bigger gain just makes the jump bigger.
4. **Noise and drift are small.** With no load, readings wobble about ±5 counts. The back sensor's drift earlier, with the plate on, was about 10 counts. Next to a 57-count preload that's minor. Rubber stoppers do slowly creep under load, though, so they're worth watching in the drift test.

**Things I'm correcting:**
- **The sensors aren't FSR 402s.** Square sensors of that size marked A502 are almost certainly Tekscan FlexiForce A502s. The CDR is out of date, and I've updated my notes.
- **Load cells are off the table, as Mason said.** Dropping that question.

**Next, if you want it:** a quick, cheap fix to try is giving the front sensor a little preload. For example, a thin shim under the front stopper so it always presses lightly. Then front and back both start above the dead zone.

**Two quick tests:**
- With the plate back on, press each of the four contact points with the same object, like a full water bottle. Each sensor's reading for the same weight tells us whether the gains are compensating for something physical.
- I still can't see the left and right sensors at all, because their gains are 0.01. Uploading tonight's firmware gives me raw counts from all four. It has the two safety fixes and the new telemetry lines, and it's ready whenever you power the chair off for USB.

**Mason explained the 370: they measured about 370 with the plate on and subtracted it as the floor.** I dug into the code. There are actually two numbers:
- **400 is what gets subtracted before calibration.** It's a dashboard setting, the default sensor floor.
- **370 is what the result gets divided by before calibration.** It's the starting "weight" estimate.

And today's numbers show **372 is just what the sensors read with zero force**. With the plate off, the front sensor read 372. With it on, 373. So the 370 you measured with the plate on was really the amplifier's baseline, not the plate's weight. That makes 400 sit about 28 counts above true zero, which is why the front reading sits negative with nobody in the chair. Calibration replaces both numbers, so this matters before calibrating and for the seat detection in #11.

And Charles, noted on curiosity: when a number like that comes up, I'll go look it up in the code myself.

**Mason on tightening the top-plate bolts by eye:** that might be a bigger deal than it sounds. The bolt tension decides how much each sensor is pressed before anyone sits down. Since it's set by eye every time the plate goes back on, every session starts with different preloads. One bolt a quarter-turn tighter, and that sensor starts higher, so the same gains feel different. **That's a plausible cause of "it felt different today."**

Two cheap fixes:
- **Spacers or shoulder bolts**, so the bolts stop at a fixed height and can't press the plate down however tight they are.
- **Check every time the plate goes back on:** leave the seat empty for 30 seconds and I'll compare all four readings against tonight's. One minute, and you'll know right away whether the preload changed.

**Charles, on needing data with someone sitting in it:** agreed. That's the next step. I'll take a few minutes with someone sitting still, then leaning forward, back, left and right, holding each lean, and I'll pull the numbers. Front and back I can read right away. Left and right will look nearly flat until the new firmware is uploaded, because their gains are 0.01.

**Charles asked what else to try, given the worries about consistency, stopping, and leaning back.**

**First, my read on leaning back.** Mason's trick of calibrating zero while leaning a bit forward is the key clue. It suggests the back sensor never really sees "lean back." When the rider leans back, his torso's weight goes into the **backrest**, which the seat sensors can't feel. What the seat does feel is the **front sensor unloading**, and for a bigger rider, the hips lifting and pressing the front sensor instead. So I'd stop trying to make the back sensor work. Two signals to test instead:
- **"Front lighter than neutral" means reverse.** That's what Mason's trick does without meaning to. It also makes the hip thrust less of a problem, because then it only matters relative to neutral.
- **Total seat load dropping means weight went into the backrest.** That's a signal by itself, and it can't come from the hips, because lifting your hips doesn't take weight off the seat.

The data will settle which one works. That's the first test below.

**What I'd do tonight, in order, all in the indoor space with the wheels off:**

1. **Upload tonight's firmware.** It has the two safety fixes and the raw-sensor telemetry. Without it I can't see left and right at all, and calibration work is guesswork. **This is code that drives the motors, so I'm asking first: OK to upload?** Power off, plug in USB, I run the upload, unplug, power on.

   - sit still 10 s;
   - hold forward, back, left, right for 5 s each, three times;

**Results from Mason's run, bolts as before.** I can only see front and back for now, in raw counts, where 372 means zero force:

| What Mason did | Front | Back |
|---|---|---|
| Empty seat | 378 | 431 |
| Sitting, relaxed | **~380 (basically zero)** | ~660 |
| Leaning forward | 460–600 | drops to ~600 |
| Leaning back **into** the backrest | 372 | ~815 |
| Leaning back **just short of** the backrest | 372 | **~970** |
| "Active neutral" (leaning slightly forward) | 372 | **~870** |
| Relaxed again | ~390 | ~700 |

**What this says:**

1. **With Mason sitting relaxed, the front sensor feels nothing.** It only wakes up when he leans forward. So it can't tell you anything about leaning back, and the entire backward signal comes from the back sensor.
2. **The back sensor does see a back lean, even with the bolts as they were**: 150 to 300 counts. The sensor isn't blind.
3. **Charles's idea of a small lean is right.** A small lean back puts *more* load on the back sensor than leaning all the way into the backrest, about 970 against 815. The backrest takes weight the seat would otherwise feel. the rider should lean back only until the backrest starts pushing back.
4. **The big one: the back sensor doesn't come back down after a back lean.** After those leans, it sat about 200 counts above its starting neutral for two full minutes, including through "active neutral." It only came down when Mason shifted at 21:27:59. **If you calibrate while it's stuck high, most of the back-lean range is gone, and leaning back "does nothing."** That would explain Mason's trick: leaning forward unsticks it, so the calibration catches a low back reading. It would also explain the inconsistency, because neutral depends on whatever you did just before.

**Whether the plate is catching on the bolts or the sensor and rubber are slowly creeping is exactly what the loose-bolt run will tell us.** If the plate is catching, the stuck-high reading should mostly go away with loose bolts. Same sequence, same rider: sit relaxed 10 s, forward ×3, small back lean ×3, then relaxed for 30 s at the end so I can see whether it comes back down. Loosen them when you're ready.

**Mason asked what the back sensor does when you lean forward, and the front sensor when you lean back.**
- **Leaning forward:** the back sensor drops a little, from about 665 to about 600, while the front climbs to 460–600.
- **Leaning back:** the front sensor doesn't move. It's already at zero when you sit relaxed, and it can't go below zero. **So for a back lean the front sensor tells us nothing, and the back sensor is doing all the work.**
- Mason's point about his butt lifting once he pushes into the backrest matches the data too: leaning hard into the backrest put *less* load on the back sensor than the small lean did.

**Charles's seesaw idea agrees with that.** If the plate tips on the line through the two side sensors, and your center of mass sits behind that line, you get exactly what we saw: the front sensor unloaded at neutral, and everything resting on the back sensor. Moving the side sensors back, under where you actually sit, would leave weight on the front sensor at neutral. Then a front sensor that goes lighter becomes a second back-lean signal. That's the strongest case for drilling the new holes. Whether it matters after calibration is something the data can settle: test it once the holes exist.

**Charles asked what I see. First, a correction to what I said after Mason's last run:** the back sensor wasn't really stuck high. Most of that was Mason sitting upright between the leans. It took this run to catch it.

**Loose bolts vs. bolts as before, same rider and same moves.** Raw back-sensor counts:

| Move | Bolts as before | Bolts loose |
|---|---|---|
| Empty seat | 431 | ~405, slowly settling upward |
| Slouched | 655–680 | 680–726 |
| **Sitting tall** | 790–920 | 802–859 |
| Lean back into backrest | 775–855 | 770–853 |
| Lean back until backrest touches | 940–1004 | 918–968 |
| Forward | 590–670 (front 460–600) | 669–674 (front 533) |
| Slouched again | 680–735 | 714–732 |

1. **The bolts weren't the problem.** Loosening them took about 25 counts off the empty-seat preload, and the leans read the same as before. Charles's ear was right: the plate isn't really catching. The binding idea doesn't hold at these settings. It was worth ruling out.
2. **The real consistency problem: sitting tall puts 100–150 more counts on the back sensor than slouching.** A full back lean is only about 250 over slouching. **So switching between your two "neutrals" moves the reading about half as much as a deliberate back lean.** Calibrate slouched and then sit up tall, and the chair sees a half back lean. Calibrate tall and then slouch, and it sees a forward lean. That alone could explain "worked great, then didn't."
3. **Repeated in both runs:** the front sensor reads zero except when leaning forward. A small back lean, just until the backrest touches, beats leaning hard into the backrest.
4. **Mason's trick, explained:** leaning forward before calibrating resets you to a low back reading, like a slouch. That's why it gave him back-lean range.

**What this means:**
- Pick **one** neutral, probably sitting tall, since that's how the rider will hold himself to drive. Calibrate in it, and coach it.
- Or make the math stop caring which neutral you're in. Moving the side sensors back, the seesaw idea, would help, because then the front sensor carries load at neutral too.

- **`left`**, **`right`**, and **`reseat`**.

Both runs are now on the same axes.

**One thing the chart shows that we hadn't caught, and it cuts against calibrating in active neutral:** measured from **active neutral**, leaning back until the backrest touches only adds about **100 counts** on the back sensor. Leaning into the backrest and pivoting reads *lower* than active neutral. Measured from **passive neutral**, the same leans add about 280. So if you calibrate sitting upright:
- the back-lean range is small, and
- leaning hard into the backrest reads as a slight **forward** lean.

That's the "lean back, nothing happens, or it goes forward" complaint, and you don't need the hip thrust to explain it. **Coaching one neutral won't fix this alone. The back sensor simply doesn't have much room above active neutral.**

**Charles, you asked me to lead the sensor-position study. Here's the plan.** It's also written into the catalog.

**Physics first.** The plate tips on the line through the two side sensors. The rider's center of mass sits *behind* that line, so the plate tips back and the front sensor lifts off to zero. **None of the existing holes can change that.** Moving front or back, or sliding the side sensors in or out along their arm, doesn't move that line. Only new holes that put the side sensors 2–3" behind center would. With the holes we have:

- **E4, back sensor moved out from B2 to B5** (F5 B5 L5 R5). A longer lever on the back sensor should give a bigger back-lean signal. The real question is whether the difference between back lean and active neutral grows, or the two just scale up together.
- **E5, front preload.** Tighten only the front bolt so the front sensor reads about +100 with the seat empty. If it then drops when you lean back, the front sensor becomes a second back-lean signal. That's what Mason's lean-forward calibration trick does without meaning to, and it costs zero drilling.
- **Later:** drill side holes behind center for the real seesaw test.

**Each change:** 30 s empty seat first (preload check), then I call the moves:

> passive-neutral 10 s → active-neutral 10 s → forward ×2 → back-min ×2 → back-touch ×2 → back-pivot ×1 → left ×2 → right ×2 → passive-neutral 10 s → reseat ×2

- **A slow sweep in each direction.** Start from active neutral and count out loud as you lean slowly and evenly over a 5-count to full, hold 2, come back over a 5-count. Forward, back (to back-touch), left, right, twice each. About 2 minutes.
- **The chart:** for each direction, the center of pressure plotted against how far into the lean you are, 0–100%, all four on one graph. Underneath each, a **sensitivity strip** showing how fast the signal changes at that point. Flat sections are dead zones, where you lean and nothing happens. Steep sections are where the chair gets twitchy. That shows exactly where the rider's small movements are lost.
- **For a better measure of lean later:** strap a phone to the rider's chest with a free sensor-logging app like phyphox, so the x-axis becomes the actual torso angle instead of an even count. Cheap, and it removes the guesswork.

**For that, I added a 20-times-a-second raw-sensor line.** It's telemetry only, built, tested and pushed. One reading a second, what we have now, can't show a 5-second transition. Under your rule it's an upload I can just do: **power off, plug in USB, say go.** It takes about a minute.

3. **E5**, back sensor to B5: sweeps again, then compare the two maps on one chart.

**First, a problem: my data tap stopped at 23:21.** Background jobs here are capped at 2 hours, and it hit the cap. Nothing since 23:21 has been recorded. **Want me to restart it?** It'll run up to another 2 hours. Before 23:21 everything is saved, through E6 and the minutes after it.

**Charles noticed that the left and right sensors alone tell you all four directions. That's right, and it's the clearest way anyone's put it tonight:**
- **Both rising** means forward. **Both dropping** means back. **One up and one down** means left or right.
- That's two simple sums: **left + right** gives forward/back, and **right − left** gives turning. Center of pressure does the same calculation, divided by the total weight so rider weight and seating drop out.
- So your conclusion holds: the plus layout assumed each sensor runs its own direction, but on this plate, the side sensors say more about forward and back than the front and back sensors do.

**My view on the hardware: go to a square.** Four sensors at the corners, like a bathroom-scale balance board, which is the standard way to measure center of pressure:
- **Every sensor contributes to both axes:** forward/back = front pair − back pair, turn = right pair − left pair.
- **It removes the seesaw.** The plus tips on the line through the two side sensors, and that line is what keeps the front sensor at zero. Four corners have no single line to tip on.
- **Put the square's center under where people actually sit,** which on this seat is behind the current center.

**For now:** do it in software. Center of pressure using all four sensors works with the current plus layout, and it's what tonight's data says fixes most of the feel problems. It's a behavior change, so it needs a flag and a wheels-off test before anyone rides it. The square is a fabrication job: either drill a new hole pattern or make a new plate.

**The front-sensor test** (F5 → F2) is still worth doing if you have time, once the tap is back. It checks the seesaw explanation one more time before you commit to the square.

## Rounds

| Round | Commit reviewed | Opus 5.5 | Fable 5.2 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Earlier audit (the meeting notes) | `8b0b406` (base) | 5/10 | 4/10 |
| 1 | `086fa40` | 6.5/10 | not run |
| 2 | `b618750` | 7/10 | not run |
| 3 | `3b24998` | 7/10 | not run |
| 4 | `bac4d59` | 7/10 | not run |
| 5 | `88946ba` | 7.5/10 | not run |
| 6 (one-off) | `5dbe365` | not run | 7/10 |
| 7 | `35e96ca` | 7.5/10 | not run |

**Final:** branch `council/firmware-9` at `0585006`, 14 commits on top of `8b0b406`. Not pushed, not merged, never uploaded.

The head commit is unreviewed. It holds the two fixes round 7 asked for: the remote stop button, which that reviewer named as the gap to an 8, and the speed-limit step when engaging.

The score stayed flat because each fresh reviewer found a different real problem, and I agreed with nearly all of them. Two were regressions from my own previous fix (a wrong-way pivot from the reverse lock, and a brake grab when arming on a rolling chair). One commit (`7946e83`) went in with a failing simulation assertion and was corrected in the next.

## What changed and why

**Safety**
- **Stops:** every loss of drive now brakes (speed ramp, then brake current) instead of coasting. A stop never commands above wheel speed.
- **Holding:** a chair nothing is driving is held with brake current. It is not held in Assist, or in DISARM with a rider seated. After a fault it stays held until DISARM is pressed.
- **Faults:** a silent VESC (80 ms), a sensor at an ADC rail, and the remote's stop button all disarm, in every mode. A broken sensor wire can no longer create motion.
- **Watchdog and boot:** 0.5 s watchdog; the chair always powers on in DISARM and brakes if it is rolling.
- **Seat check:** one unit, a fixed threshold, hysteresis and hold times.
- **DISARM:** always stops, however long it is held; it can no longer slide into Assist.

**Control and turning**
- **Lean math:** each direction is the load gained on that sensor pair, divided by a per-direction range. I did not use centre of pressure: the log shows total load more than doubles in a lean, which would turn a forward lean into a turn.
- **Turning:** a rate (160 deg/s at full lean) on every speed-knob position, smoothed separately from forward speed, capped at speed by sideways acceleration.
- **Sitting up:** stops the chair. Wheels inside the VESC's minimum-speed zone get brake current, and the command can no longer run ahead of the wheels.
- **Reverse:** half speed. A back lean that starts while rolling forward only brakes.
- **Reverse-turn threshold:** −0.3 is still the centre, but it fades instead of stepping, and it no longer applies while the chair still rolls forward (it steered the wrong way under braking).
- **REMOTE:** goes through the same turn and reverse limits, and needs a centred stick first.

**Calibration**
- **Neutral:** averaged over a still second after the button is released, not a 40 ms snapshot during the press.
- **Range calibration:** hold 1 s; replaces dynamic calibration, is bounded per direction, and is saved.
- **Tare:** in DISARM with the seat empty, hold 2 s; measures sensor zeros.
- **Persistence:** both survive power cycles and profile loads.
- **Drift:** neutral slowly follows sensor drift at rest, bounded at 15%.

**Tuning**
- **Removed:** per-sensor gains, the turn-mix curve, calibration sample count, the shared floor and the startup-mode setting.
- **Added:** four ranges, four zeros, seat threshold, turn rate, sideways limit, track width and VESC minimum speed.
- **Dashboard:** edits apply immediately. It shows the ranges and a health badge; this part was built by an Opus 5.5 subagent and I reviewed the diff.

**Other**
- **Assist:** redesigned so it cannot drive itself. A boost must be earned by a push and fades in 0.7 s.
- **Structure:** `DriveController` holds the logic with no hardware dependency, so whole rides run on the host, including 270 randomized rides checked against safety rules every tick.
- **VESC reference:** I saved three upstream VESC source files to `~/LEAN/vesc-firmware-ref/` (outside the repo).

## Test wheels-off first

Nearly everything about how the chair drives has changed. In order:
1. **VESC Tool settings** (listed at the top of `src/drive_constants.h`):
   - "Allow Braking" on.
   - Timeout 200 ms, with 15 A timeout brake current.
   - CAN status at 50 Hz.
   - Note the VESC firmware version.
2. **Minimum ERPM.** Lower `s_pid_min_erpm` and set "VESC minimum speed" to match. At the default, a small lean does nothing and then the chair starts at 0.5 m/s. Every reviewer called this the real cause of "nothing, then everything".
3. **Brake current.** This CAN command has never been sent on this chair. Check a DISARM stop from each knob position.
4. **Tare, then range calibration**, before any lean test. Default ranges are guesses from one log.
5. **Direction check:** lean right, and the left wheel must run faster.
6. **Watchdog:** confirm upload still works and that a forced hang reports `WATCHDOG_RESET`.
7. **By feel:**
   - turn stop sharpness (1800 deg/s²);
   - the hold when pushing the chair;
   - the reverse lock;
   - Assist, which is fail-quiet: if push detection doesn't trigger on real wheels, it does nothing.

## What reviewers kept flagging that I didn't resolve

- **VESC minimum-speed dead zone** (every round): only fixable on the bench. The firmware works around it for stopping and logs a hint.
- **`main.cpp` has no host tests** (every round): it isn't host-compiled; fixing that means moving the profile and settings-gate logic out.
- **Spec B** (most rounds): the speed command ends 0.23, 0.56, 0.89 and 1.22 s after a stop begins, by knob position. Meeting 0.5 s from top speed needs 8.7 m/s², more than the motors can deliver.
- **LEAN needs a fresh calibration on every entry**, even after a quick DISARM. I kept it: it is your design and it fails safe.
- **One EEPROM bank:** a power cut mid-save resets settings. It is now logged at boot, but a second bank doesn't fit beside seven profiles.
- **Braking out of reverse** un-flips the turn at once. Low severity; left alone.
- **Disagreed with:**
  - acting on DISARM before debounce;
  - an integral term on the hold current;
  - scaling turn rate with the speed knob.

## Questions for the team

1. **Stop rate:** keep 3.7 m/s² and relax Spec B, or raise it?
2. **Motors:** do they have hall sensors? That decides how low the minimum ERPM can go.
3. **Track width:** I guessed 0.60 m; the turn rate scales with it.
4. **Turn rate:** 160 deg/s default, against Spec H's 200. Sideways limit is 2.5 m/s².
5. **Holding:** is "held when empty, free with a rider in DISARM, tap DISARM to push the empty chair" the behaviour you want?
6. **Sensor peak:** does a hard lean from your heaviest rider get near 4000 raw counts? That would trip the rail fault mid-ride.
7. **Seat threshold:** 200 counts, 80 ms to read empty. Does Mason register as seated, and do bumps trip it?
8. **REMOTE with an empty seat** keeps driving. Intended?
9. **Remote packets** still have no checksum; fixing it means reflashing the controller too.
10. **First rides:** cap the speed knob in software?

**The review loop finished without reaching 8/10.** Scores were 6.5 → 7 → 7 → 7 → 7.5, then a single Fable review at 7, then 7.5. Each round's reviewer found a different real problem rather than repeating complaints. Two of those problems came from the loop's own earlier fixes. It's on branch `council/firmware-9`, only on this laptop, never uploaded: 54 files and about 6,750 lines changed against `charles/dev-setup`. The last commit was never reviewed.

**What it changed, briefly:**
- **Stopping and faults:** every way of stopping now actively brakes instead of coasting, and a chair with nobody driving it is held in place. A silent motor controller, a sensor at its limit, and the remote's stop button all disarm, in every mode. A watchdog resets the Teensy if the program freezes. The chair always powers on in Disarm.
- **Steering:** a lean sets a turn rate (160°/s at full lean) regardless of the speed knob, smoothed separately from speed. Reverse is limited to half speed.
- **Calibration:**
  - Neutral is averaged over a second after you release the button.
  - A new range calibration replaces Caleb's dynamic one.
  - A tare (zeroing) step runs with the seat empty.
  - All of it is saved through power-off.
- **Tuning:** the per-sensor gains are gone, and dashboard edits take effect immediately.

**Where it disagrees with what we measured tonight:** it chose **not** to use center of pressure. Its reasoning: total load more than doubles during a lean, so dividing by it would turn a forward lean into a turn. Our data partly backs that. In E4, a forward lean moved the side-to-side reading from 0.19 to 0.12, so there is some leakage. But center of pressure is also what fixed the two-neutrals problem. Both approaches can work, so this should be settled on the chair, not by reviewer opinion.

**Charles's "Claude vs Caleb" test: don't ride this yet.** Almost everything about how the chair drives has changed, including braking commands this chair has never received. Its list of what to test first, in order:
1. VESC Tool settings, including the minimum-ERPM setting.
2. A Disarm stop from each speed knob position, wheels off.
3. Tare, then range calibration.
4. Lean right and confirm the left wheel runs faster.
5. Then feel.

That's a session of its own, not a late-night add-on.

**Some of its questions I can answer:**
- Hall sensors: yes, according to the old CLAUDE.md.

**The tap is back**, restarted at about 23:30. As you said, the gap since 23:21 doesn't matter.

**Charles asked for proven numbers comparing the current math with one that uses all four sensors.** The chart above runs the same real sweeps, from the B6 run, through four versions of the math. Here's what it doesn't spell out:

- **Smarter math doesn't buy more resolution.** I expected combining the sensors to add some. It doesn't: forward stays at about 10 steps, back about 6, side to side about 27, whichever sensible version you use. The wobble that limits it is Mason's body swaying slightly while he sits still, and that sway shows up on all four sensors at once, so combining them can't cancel it. **To get finer control, change the hardware** (square layout, the seesaw) **or how the rider sits** (stabilizing the torso), not the math.
- **What the math does decide is whether that resolution gets used.** The current firmware with tonight's settings wastes most of it:
  - lean steering is effectively switched off;
  - forward goes from nothing to full speed in the first third of the lean, which is the on/off feel you've described;
  - back can never reach full reverse.

  Using all four sensors spreads every direction smoothly from zero to full across the whole lean.
- **The review loop's version of the math is close to the "all-sensor sums" line** (yellow in the chart), and this data supports it. So the center-of-pressure vs. sensor-pairs disagreement is smaller than it sounded: both use every sensor well. Center of pressure has one extra advantage: it cancels rider weight and posture. That's the E4 result.

**Mason, on the plus layout:** for what it's worth, the plus wasn't a dumb call. You could only see what was wrong with it once the raw side-sensor data existed, and that happened tonight. Charles's lesson is the right one: big design calls deserve a quick data check before everyone signs off.

**Saved:** the full comparison is in `experiments/README.md`, with the script, so anyone can rerun it on new data.

I didn't touch git state. To check the agent's claim about tests, I exported the branch to `/tmp` and ran the host tests there: all 12 native suites pass.

**What the page shows:**
1. **Stopping and faults:** a before/after speed-over-time chart for each case: seat empty, DISARM, remote lost, silent motor controller, sensor short, sensor wire break, Teensy freeze, power-on while rolling, and the remote's stop button. Old code mostly coasted or kept going; the branch brakes and then holds. There's also a chart of a stop's phases, the stop-time bars against the 0.5 s spec, and a grid of when the chair is held vs free.
2. **LEAN never coasted, before or after.** Sitting up has always made the motors slow the chair. The branch only adds brake current for the last bit, then holds. Now the only places it coasts are Assist and DISARM with a rider seated, after the stop. Pressing DISARM at speed now brakes at about 0.38 g. The basketball callout says this plainly.
3. **Steering:** turn rate is 160°/s on every speed-knob setting (it used to scale with the knob, 80–318°/s). It's capped at speed by a sideways-acceleration limit, and steering no longer drops out while speeding up. Reverse is half speed, and a back lean while rolling forward only brakes to a stop. The reverse turn flip now fades instead of jumping.
4. **Calibration and settings:** step-by-step diagrams for neutral, range and tare, plus what survives a power-off. Sensor zeros and lean ranges are saved; neutral is not. Also the new lean math, the seat-check hysteresis, and chips for every setting removed, added or tightened, plus the dashboard's new health badge.
5. **Assist and testing:** Assist is now one push, one fading boost, with no runaway. There's a 14-step wheels-off checklist, mostly the VESC Tool settings the code relies on, and 8 open questions for the team.

**Where the code contradicts the agent's summary:**
- **"Dashboard live edits" isn't in the diff.** Settings edits are still DISARM-only, and are now also refused while the chair is braking or the wheels are turning. What the dashboard actually gained is a health badge and a lean-range readout.
- **"Faults disarm" is only half the story.**
  - In Assist, a sensor or motor-controller fault just drops the boost: no braking, no hold.
  - A broken (open) sensor wire isn't detected at all. Only shorts trip the fault. The new math does stop a broken wire from causing motion.
- **The watchdog doesn't brake during a freeze.** It restarts the Teensy after 0.5 s. Until then, the VESCs' own timeout settings decide what happens, and the firmware can't set those. With VESC defaults the chair keeps its last speed command for up to about 0.5 s.
- **"Brakes instead of coasting" needs two caveats.**
  - Normal LEAN driving never coasted in the old code either.
  - The header comment in `StopController.h` still says a stop ends with the chair rolling freely. In most cases the branch now holds it instead.
- **The summary leaves out that stops miss the 0.5 s spec on knobs 2–4.** The agent's own code comment admits it (0.56, 0.89 and 1.22 s).
- Minor: a comment in `Registry.h` points to `Persistence::saveSelected`, which doesn't exist. The real function is `saveCalibration`.

The other claims hold up: holding, the 80 ms detection, seat-check hysteresis, turn as a rate, half-speed reverse, neutral averaged after you let go, saved range and tare, per-sensor gains removed, the Assist redesign, and the DriveController host tests.

**Charles's sweeps: two things stand out.**

1. **Where your feet are moves your weight about 1.5" forward.** With your feet on the footrest, your neutral sat about 2" behind the center. With them hanging, it moved to roughly the side-sensor line, and when you leaned forward the front sensor finally loaded. **The footrest height is a hidden setting on the seesaw problem.** the rider's legs will be dead weight on that footrest, so its height decides where his weight sits. Set it on purpose, write it down, and test with it.
2. **Both of you lean further right than left.** Charles reached +1.9" right but only −0.6" left, and Mason was right-heavy too. **Two different riders with the same bias suggests the sensors aren't reading alike**, not how either of you sits. That's exactly what the jug test checks, so good timing.

Also from your run: your back lean barely moves the center of pressure, about 0.3–0.5" against about 1.4" forward. Back is weak for a bigger rider too. And you're right that tense legs change it. With your legs relaxed, which is closer to how the rider will sit, you end up pivoting on the backrest.

**For the square, covering both riders and both foot positions:** center about 1.2" behind the current center, with corners about 5" out. Mason didn't follow the "deadzone and full command" line. In plain terms, the firmware needs two numbers for each direction:
- **Deadzone:** how much wobble around neutral still counts as "stopped."
- **Full command:** how far you have to lean before it's full speed.

Set both from how far you actually lean, not from where the sensors sit. For Mason today, that's about 2" forward of active neutral for full forward and about 1.9" behind it for full reverse. The rectangle's edges are 4–5" out, and nobody leans that far.

Charles asked whether the jug is heavy enough:
- **For the bare-sensor test, it is.** It gave 54–115 counts against 1–2 counts of noise. The trouble last night was how the weight touched the sensor, not how heavy it was.
- **For a plate-on test, go heavier: 25–40 lb** (dumbbells, a bucket of water, a backpack of books). On the plate the weight splits four ways, and a 9 lb jug would only place the weight to within about ±0.4".

The plate-on test is the one that matters. Set the weight on the plate over each corner and at the centre. If the math puts it where you set it, the gains are good enough, however the bare sensors read.

Charles said left/right seating is random anyway. Agreed: the right-bias question doesn't matter. Gains matter less than I made them sound, too. If calibration records each rider's neutral and full lean in each direction, that cancels most of a sensor reading high or low. So that's how I'd build the corner math: **per-rider calibration first, per-sensor gains as a small correction.**

Do one quick repeat of the bare-sensor test so we know whether back-right is actually weak. If it is, check its wiring or mounting before we correct for it in software.
